People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. It has to be in a set order, or it doesn’t feel like it counts. I’ll reopen the spreadsheet, go through the emails line by line, then check the locks or the payment confirmation again. If I get interrupted or I’m not sure I did it “right,” I start over. It’s like I’m trying to get to this feeling that it’s safe, but some nights I’m still at my desk hours later and nothing is actually finished.

It’s like I’m trying to get to this feeling that it’s safe, but some nights I’m still at my desk hours later and nothing is actually finished.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're unable to complete your checks in the specific way you described?\n\nPatient: Yes. If I can’t do it in the right order, I get really anxious. I’ll end up reopening the spreadsheets, rereading emails line by line, checking the locks or payment confirmations again and again until it feels safe enough. If it doesn’t feel right, I can’t settle down, and some nights I’m still at my desk hours later with nothing actually finished.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever feel the need to perform these checks or rituals when you're not feeling anxious?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 53 / mathematical_science_occupation / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Compulsions\nmanifestation: \"To calm myself down I end up reopening spreadsheets, rereading emails line by line, checking locks and payment confirmations over and over in a set order until it feels 'safe,' and some nights I’m still at my desk hours later with nothing actually finished.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]

Yes, sometimes I do. It’s not always like I’m already panicking first. Sometimes I just feel this pull to check anyway, like I need to make sure it’s safe before I can move on.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I keep getting these awful unwanted thoughts that I’ve missed something important. Like a wrong number in a data release, or that I left the back door unlocked, or paid a bill from the wrong account. Even when I know I checked, my mind just won’t let go of the idea that I might have caused some kind of disaster.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel the need to do anything to make these unwanted thoughts go away?\n\nPatient: Yes. I try to make the thought quiet by checking. I’ll reopen spreadsheets, reread emails line by line, check the door locks, look at payment confirmations again and again. I do it in a certain order until it feels safe enough. Sometimes I’m still sitting at my desk hours later, and I haven’t actually finished anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do these checks in a very specific way?\n\nPatient: Yes. It has to be in a set order, or it doesn’t feel like it counts. I’ll reopen the spreadsheet, go through the emails line by line, then check the locks or the payment confirmation again. If I get interrupted or I’m not sure I did it “right,” I start over. It’s like I’m trying to get to this feeling that it’s safe, but some nights I’m still at my desk hours later and nothing is actually finished.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're unable to complete your checks in the specific way you described?\n\nPatient: Yes. If I can’t do it in the right order, I get really anxious. I’ll end up reopening the spreadsheets, rereading emails line by line, checking the locks or payment confirmations again and again until it feels safe enough. If it doesn’t feel right, I can’t settle down, and some nights I’m still at my desk hours later with nothing actually finished.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to perform these checks or rituals when you're not feeling anxious?\n\nPatient: Yes, sometimes I do. It’s not always like I’m already panicking first. Sometimes I just feel this pull to check anyway, like I need to make sure it’s safe before I can move on. So I’ll reopen the spreadsheet, reread the emails, check the locks or payment confirmations in the same order. If I don’t do it, or if it doesn’t feel right, then I start getting worked up.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these unwanted thoughts when you're feeling calm and not worried about anything in particular?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s part of what bothers me. I can be sitting there feeling pretty calm, and then the thought just pops in—like, what if I missed a wrong number in a data release, or the back door isn’t really locked, or I paid a bill from the wrong account. Even if I know I already checked, my mind won’t let go of the idea that I might have caused something serious.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and checking behaviors affecting your ability to work or take care of daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I’ve fallen behind on some major work deadlines because I’ll stay late reviewing things over and over, but somehow still not actually finish them. And outside of work, I’ve stopped making it to things like salsa nights or kayaking because I can’t get away from the checking and reviewing in time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. That’s part of what bothers me. I can be sitting there feeling pretty calm, and then the thought just pops in—like, what if I missed a wrong number in a data release, or the back door isn’t really locked, or I paid a bill from the wrong account. Even if I know I already checked, my mind won’t let go of the idea that I might have caused something serious.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and checking behaviors affecting your ability to work or take care of daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I’ve fallen behind on some major work deadlines because I’ll stay late reviewing things over and over, but somehow still not actually finish them. And outside of work, I’ve stopped making it to things like salsa nights or kayaking because I can’t get away from the checking and reviewing in time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checks are necessary to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes. In the moment it really feels like I have to do them to stop something bad from happening. I’ll reopen spreadsheets, reread emails line by line, check locks or payment confirmations over and over, usually in the same order, until it feels safe. If I don’t get that feeling, I keep going, and some nights I’m still at my desk hours later with nothing actually finished.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I keep getting these awful unwanted thoughts that I’ve missed something important. Like a wrong number in a data release, or that I left the back door unlocked, or paid a bill from the wrong account. Even when I know I checked, my mind just won’t let go of the idea that I might have caused some kind of disaster.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel the need to do anything to make these unwanted thoughts go away?\n\nPatient: Yes. I try to make the thought quiet by checking. I’ll reopen spreadsheets, reread emails line by line, check the door locks, look at payment confirmations again and again. I do it in a certain order until it feels safe enough. Sometimes I’m still sitting at my desk hours later, and I haven’t actually finished anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do these checks in a very specific way?\n\nPatient: Yes. It has to be in a set order, or it doesn’t feel like it counts. I’ll reopen the spreadsheet, go through the emails line by line, then check the locks or the payment confirmation again. If I get interrupted or I’m not sure I did it “right,” I start over. It’s like I’m trying to get to this feeling that it’s safe, but some nights I’m still at my desk hours later and nothing is actually finished.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're unable to complete your checks in the specific way you described?\n\nPatient: Yes. If I can’t do it in the right order, I get really anxious. I’ll end up reopening the spreadsheets, rereading emails line by line, checking the locks or payment confirmations again and again until it feels safe enough. If it doesn’t feel right, I can’t settle down, and some nights I’m still at my desk hours later with nothing actually finished.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to perform these checks or rituals when you're not feeling anxious?\n\nPatient: Yes, sometimes I do. It’s not always like I’m already panicking first. Sometimes I just feel this pull to check anyway, like I need to make sure it’s safe before I can move on. So I’ll reopen the spreadsheet, reread the emails, check the locks or payment confirmations in the same order. If I don’t do it, or if it doesn’t feel right, then I start getting worked up.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these unwanted thoughts when you're feeling calm and not worried about anything in particular?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s part of what bothers me. I can be sitting there feeling pretty calm, and then the thought just pops in—like, what if I missed a wrong number in a data release, or the back door isn’t really locked, or I paid a bill from the wrong account. Even if I know I already checked, my mind won’t let go of the idea that I might have caused something serious.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and checking behaviors affecting your ability to work or take care of daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I’ve fallen behind on some major work deadlines because I’ll stay late reviewing things over and over, but somehow still not actually finish them. And outside of work, I’ve stopped making it to things like salsa nights or kayaking because I can’t get away from the checking and reviewing in time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checks are necessary to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes. In the moment it really feels like I have to do them to stop something bad from happening. I’ll reopen spreadsheets, reread emails line by line, check locks or payment confirmations over and over, usually in the same order, until it feels safe. If I don’t get that feeling, I keep going, and some nights I’m still at my desk hours later with nothing actually finished.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these unwanted thoughts are part of your own thoughts and beliefs, or do they feel like they're coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: They feel like they’re coming from me—like they’re my own thoughts—but I don’t want them. It’s not like I hear someone else saying it or anything. It’s more that my mind grabs onto this possibility, like maybe I missed a wrong number or left the back door unlocked, and even though part of me knows I checked, I can’t get it to let go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these unwanted thoughts align with your own values and beliefs?\n\nPatient: No, they don’t really line up with what I believe. So I’ll reopen the spreadsheet, reread the emails, check the locks or payment confirmations in the same order. If I don’t do it, or if it doesn’t feel right, then I start getting worked up.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these unwanted thoughts when you're feeling calm and not worried about anything in particular?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s part of what bothers me. I can be sitting there feeling pretty calm, and then the thought just pops in—like, what if I missed a wrong number in a data release, or the back door isn’t really locked, or I paid a bill from the wrong account. Even if I know I already checked, my mind won’t let go of the idea that I might have caused something serious.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and checking behaviors affecting your ability to work or take care of daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I’ve fallen behind on some major work deadlines because I’ll stay late reviewing things over and over, but somehow still not actually finish them. And outside of work, I’ve stopped making it to things like salsa nights or kayaking because I can’t get away from the checking and reviewing in time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checks are necessary to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes. In the moment it really feels like I have to do them to stop something bad from happening. I’ll reopen spreadsheets, reread emails line by line, check locks or payment confirmations over and over, usually in the same order, until it feels safe. If I don’t get that feeling, I keep going, and some nights I’m still at my desk hours later with nothing actually finished.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these unwanted thoughts are part of your own thoughts and beliefs, or do they feel like they're coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: They feel like they’re coming from me—like they’re my own thoughts—but I don’t want them. It’s not like I hear someone else saying it or anything. It’s more that my mind grabs onto this possibility, like maybe I missed a wrong number or left the back door unlocked, and even though part of me knows I checked, I can’t get it to let go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these unwanted thoughts align with your own values and beliefs?\n\nPatient: No, they don’t really line up with what I believe. I don’t want to think I’ve caused some disaster because I missed a number, or left the back door unlocked, or paid a bill from the wrong account. That’s not how I see myself. They’re just awful thoughts that get stuck, even when I know I already checked.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these thoughts are realistic or exaggerated?\n\nPatient: They’re exaggerated, I think. In the moment they feel realistic enough that I can’t ignore them, but when I step back, I know it’s not likely that one missed number or a door lock or a bill mistake means I’ve caused some huge disaster. It’s just that my mind won’t let go of that possibility.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

If I don’t get that feeling, I keep going, and some nights I’m still at my desk hours later with nothing actually finished.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these unwanted thoughts are part of your own thoughts and beliefs, or do they feel like they're coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: They feel like they’re coming from me—like they’re my own thoughts—but I don’t want them. It’s not like I hear someone else saying it or anything. It’s more that my mind grabs onto this possibility, like maybe I missed a wrong number or left the back door unlocked, and even though part of me knows I checked, I can’t get it to let go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these unwanted thoughts align with your own values and beliefs?\n\nPatient: No, they don’t really line up with what I believe. I don’t want to think I’ve caused some disaster because I missed a number, or left the back door unlocked, or paid a bill from the wrong account. That’s not how I see myself. They’re just awful thoughts that get stuck, even when I know I already checked.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these thoughts are realistic or exaggerated?\n\nPatient: They’re exaggerated, I think. In the moment they feel realistic enough that I can’t ignore them, but when I step back, I know it’s not likely that one missed number or a door lock or a bill mistake means I’ve caused some huge disaster. It’s just that my mind won’t let go of that possibility.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything unreasonable about the thoughts you're having and the behaviors you're using to cope with them?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I know it’s unreasonable when I say it out loud. Reopening the same spreadsheets, rereading emails line by line, checking the locks or payment confirmations over and over in a certain order until it feels “safe”... it doesn’t really make sense.\n\nBut in the moment it feels like I have to do it to calm down. And then some nights I’m still sitting at my desk hours later, and I haven’t actually finished anything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I keep getting these awful unwanted thoughts that I’ve missed something important. Like a wrong number in a data release, or that I left the back door unlocked, or paid a bill from the wrong account. Even when I know I checked, my mind just won’t let go of the idea that I might have caused some kind of disaster.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel the need to do anything to make these unwanted thoughts go away?\n\nPatient: Yes. I try to make the thought quiet by checking. I’ll reopen spreadsheets, reread emails line by line, check the door locks, look at payment confirmations again and again. I do it in a certain order until it feels safe enough. Sometimes I’m still sitting at my desk hours later, and I haven’t actually finished anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do these checks in a very specific way?\n\nPatient: Yes. It has to be in a set order, or it doesn’t feel like it counts. I’ll reopen the spreadsheet, go through the emails line by line, then check the locks or the payment confirmation again. If I get interrupted or I’m not sure I did it “right,” I start over. It’s like I’m trying to get to this feeling that it’s safe, but some nights I’m still at my desk hours later and nothing is actually finished.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're unable to complete your checks in the specific way you described?\n\nPatient: Yes. If I can’t do it in the right order, I get really anxious. I’ll end up reopening the spreadsheets, rereading emails line by line, checking the locks or payment confirmations again and again until it feels safe enough. If it doesn’t feel right, I can’t settle down, and some nights I’m still at my desk hours later with nothing actually finished.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to perform these checks or rituals when you're not feeling anxious?\n\nPatient: Yes, sometimes I do. It’s not always like I’m already panicking first. Sometimes I just feel this pull to check anyway, like I need to make sure it’s safe before I can move on. So I’ll reopen the spreadsheet, reread the emails, check the locks or payment confirmations in the same order. If I don’t do it, or if it doesn’t feel right, then I start getting worked up.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these unwanted thoughts when you're feeling calm and not worried about anything in particular?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s part of what bothers me. I can be sitting there feeling pretty calm, and then the thought just pops in—like, what if I missed a wrong number in a data release, or the back door isn’t really locked, or I paid a bill from the wrong account. Even if I know I already checked, my mind won’t let go of the idea that I might have caused something serious.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and checking behaviors affecting your ability to work or take care of daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I’ve fallen behind on some major work deadlines because I’ll stay late reviewing things over and over, but somehow still not actually finish them. In the moment it really feels like I have to do them to stop something bad from happening. And then some nights I’m still sitting at my desk hours later, and I haven’t actually finished anything.\n\nDoctor: How distressed do you feel when these unwanted thoughts occur?\n\nPatient: Very distressed.     "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I keep getting these awful unwanted thoughts that I’ve missed something important. Like a wrong number in a data release, or that I left the back door unlocked, or paid a bill from the wrong account. Even when I know I checked, my mind just won’t let go of the idea that I might have caused some kind of disaster.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel the need to do anything to make these unwanted thoughts go away?\n\nPatient: Yes. I try to make the thought quiet by checking. I’ll reopen spreadsheets, reread emails line by line, check the door locks, look at payment confirmations again and again. I do it in a certain order until it feels safe enough. Sometimes I’m still sitting at my desk hours later, and I haven’t actually finished anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do these checks in a very specific way?\n\nPatient: Yes. It has to be in a set order, or it doesn’t feel like it counts. I’ll reopen the spreadsheet, go through the emails line by line, then check the locks or the payment confirmation again. If I get interrupted or I’m not sure I did it “right,” I start over. It’s like I’m trying to get to this feeling that it’s safe, but some nights I’m still at my desk hours later and nothing is actually finished.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're unable to complete your checks in the specific way you described?\n\nPatient: Yes. If I can’t do it in the right order, I get really anxious. I’ll end up reopening the spreadsheets, rereading emails line by line, checking the locks or payment confirmations again and again until it feels safe enough. If it doesn’t feel right, I can’t settle down, and some nights I’m still at my desk hours later with nothing actually finished.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to perform these checks or rituals when you're not feeling anxious?\n\nPatient: Yes, sometimes I do. It’s not always like I’m already panicking first. Sometimes I just feel this pull to check anyway, like I need to make sure it’s safe before I can move on. So I’ll reopen the spreadsheet, reread the emails, check the locks or payment confirmations in the same order. If I don’t do it, or if it doesn’t feel right, then I start getting worked up.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these unwanted thoughts when you're feeling calm and not worried about anything in particular?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s part of what bothers me. I can be sitting there feeling pretty calm, and then the thought just pops in—like, what if I missed a wrong number in a data release, or the back door isn’t really locked, or I paid a bill from the wrong account. Even if I know I already checked, my mind won’t let go of the idea that I might have caused something serious.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and checking behaviors affecting your ability to work or take care of daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I’ve fallen behind on some major work deadlines because I’ll stay late reviewing things over and over, but somehow still not actually finish them. And outside of work, I’ve stopped making it to things like salsa nights or kayaking because I can’t get away from the checking and reviewing in time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checks are necessary to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes. In the moment it really feels like I have to do them to stop something bad from happening. I’ll reopen spreadsheets, reread emails line by line, check locks or payment confirmations over and over, usually in the same order, until it feels safe. If I don’t get that feeling, I keep going, and some nights I’m still at my desk hours later with nothing actually finished.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these unwanted thoughts are part of your own thoughts and beliefs, or do they feel like they're coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: They feel like they’re coming from me—like they’re my own thoughts—but I don’t want them. It’s not like I hear someone else saying it or anything. It’s more that my mind grabs onto this possibility, like maybe I missed a wrong number or left the back door unlocked, and even though part of me knows I checked, I can’t get it to let go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these unwanted thoughts align with your own values and beliefs?\n\nPatient: No, they don’t really line up with what I believe. I don’t want to think I’ve caused some disaster because I missed a number, or left the back door unlocked, or paid a bill from the wrong account. That’s not how I see myself. They’re just awful thoughts that get stuck, even when I know I already checked.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these thoughts are realistic or exaggerated?\n\nPatient: They’re exaggerated, I think. In the moment they feel realistic enough that I can’t ignore them, but when I step back, I know it’s not likely that one missed number or a door lock or a bill mistake means I’ve caused some huge disaster. It’s just that my mind won’t let go of that possibility.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything unreasonable about the thoughts you're having and the behaviors you're using to cope with them?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I know it’s unreasonable when I say it out loud. Reopening the same spreadsheets, rereading emails line by line, checking the locks or payment confirmations over and over in a certain order until it feels “safe”... it doesn’t really make sense.\n\nBut in the moment it feels like I have to do it to calm down. And then some nights I’m still sitting at my desk hours later, and I haven’t actually finished anything.\n\nDoctor: How distressed do you feel when these unwanted thoughts occur?\n\nPatient: Very distressed. When the thought hits, it feels awful—like I might have missed one critical thing, a wrong number or an unlocked door or a bill from the wrong account, and somehow that means I’ve caused a disaster. Even if I know I already checked, my mind just won’t let go of it. It can feel overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these thoughts are something you could control if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I want to control them, but they just come in anyway. I’ll know I checked the number, or locked the back door, or paid the bill from the right account, and still the thought keeps pushing back in like, what if you missed something and caused a disaster? I can sometimes try to talk myself down, but it doesn’t feel fully under my control.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I keep getting these awful unwanted thoughts that I’ve missed something important. Like a wrong number in a data release, or that I left the back door unlocked, or paid a bill from the wrong account. Even when I know I checked, my mind just won’t let go of the idea that I might have caused some kind of disaster.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel the need to do anything to make these unwanted thoughts go away?\n\nPatient: Yes. I try to make the thought quiet by checking. I’ll reopen spreadsheets, reread emails line by line, check the door locks, look at payment confirmations again and again. I do it in a certain order until it feels safe enough. Sometimes I’m still sitting at my desk hours later, and I haven’t actually finished anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do these checks in a very specific way?\n\nPatient: Yes. It has to be in a set order, or it doesn’t feel like it counts. I’ll reopen the spreadsheet, go through the emails line by line, then check the locks or the payment confirmation again. If I get interrupted or I’m not sure I did it “right,” I start over. It’s like I’m trying to get to this feeling that it’s safe, but some nights I’m still at my desk hours later and nothing is actually finished.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're unable to complete your checks in the specific way you described?\n\nPatient: Yes. If I can’t do it in the right order, I get really anxious. I’ll end up reopening the spreadsheets, rereading emails line by line, checking the locks or payment confirmations again and again until it feels safe enough. If it doesn’t feel right, I can’t settle down, and some nights I’m still at my desk hours later with nothing actually finished.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to perform these checks or rituals when you're not feeling anxious?\n\nPatient: Yes, sometimes I do. It’s not always like I’m already panicking first. Sometimes I just feel this pull to check anyway, like I need to make sure it’s safe before I can move on. So I’ll reopen the spreadsheet, reread the emails, check the locks or payment confirmations in the same order. If I don’t do it, or if it doesn’t feel right, then I start getting worked up.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these unwanted thoughts when you're feeling calm and not worried about anything in particular?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s part of what bothers me. I can be sitting there feeling pretty calm, and then the thought just pops in—like, what if I missed a wrong number in a data release, or the back door isn’t really locked, or I paid a bill from the wrong account. Even if I know I already checked, my mind won’t let go of the idea that I might have caused something serious.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and checking behaviors affecting your ability to work or take care of daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I’ve fallen behind on some major work deadlines because I’ll stay late reviewing things over and over, but somehow still not actually finish them. And outside of work, I’ve stopped making it to things like salsa nights or kayaking because I can’t get away from the checking and reviewing in time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checks are necessary to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes. In the moment it really feels like I have to do them to stop something bad from happening. I’ll reopen spreadsheets, reread emails line by line, check locks or payment confirmations over and over, usually in the same order, until it feels safe. If I don’t get that feeling, I keep going, and some nights I’m still at my desk hours later with nothing actually finished.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these unwanted thoughts are part of your own thoughts and beliefs, or do they feel like they're coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: They feel like they’re coming from me—like they’re my own thoughts—but I don’t want them. It’s not like I hear someone else saying it or anything. It’s more that my mind grabs onto this possibility, like maybe I missed a wrong number or left the back door unlocked, and even though part of me knows I checked, I can’t get it to let go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these unwanted thoughts align with your own values and beliefs?\n\nPatient: No, they don’t really line up with what I believe. I don’t want to think I’ve caused some disaster because I missed a number, or left the back door unlocked, or paid a bill from the wrong account. That’s not how I see myself. They’re just awful thoughts that get stuck, even when I know I already checked.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these thoughts are realistic or exaggerated?\n\nPatient: They’re exaggerated, I think. In the moment they feel realistic enough that I can’t ignore them, but when I step back, I know it’s not likely that one missed number or a door lock or a bill mistake means I’ve caused some huge disaster. It’s just that my mind won’t let go of that possibility.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything unreasonable about the thoughts you're having and the behaviors you're using to cope with them?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I know it’s unreasonable when I say it out loud. Reopening the same spreadsheets, rereading emails line by line, checking the locks or payment confirmations over and over in a certain order until it feels “safe”... it doesn’t really make sense.\n\nBut in the moment it feels like I have to do it to calm down. And then some nights I’m still sitting at my desk hours later, and I haven’t actually finished anything.\n\nDoctor: How distressed do you feel when these unwanted thoughts occur?\n\nPatient: Very distressed. When the thought hits, it feels awful—like I might have missed one critical thing, a wrong number or an unlocked door or a bill from the wrong account, and somehow that means I’ve caused a disaster. Even if I know I already checked, my mind just won’t let go of it. It can feel overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these thoughts are something you could control if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I want to control them, but they just come in anyway. I’ll know I checked the number, or locked the back door, or paid the bill from the right account, and still the thought keeps pushing back in like, what if you missed something and caused a disaster? I can sometimes try to talk myself down, but it doesn’t feel fully under my control.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these unwanted thoughts are a part of you, or do they feel alien to your own identity?\n\nPatient: They feel pretty alien to me. I don’t see myself as someone who wants to think this way. I came in because these awful thoughts keep pushing in—like I missed a wrong number in a data release, or left the back door unlocked, or paid a bill from the wrong account—and even after I’ve checked, my mind won’t let go of it.\n\nIt feels like it’s not really “me,” but it’s happening in my head anyway.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

They’re just awful thoughts that get stuck, even when I know I already checked.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these thoughts are realistic or exaggerated?\n\nPatient: They’re exaggerated, I think. In the moment they feel realistic enough that I can’t ignore them, but when I step back, I know it’s not likely that one missed number or a door lock or a bill mistake means I’ve caused some huge disaster. It’s just that my mind won’t let go of that possibility.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything unreasonable about the thoughts you're having and the behaviors you're using to cope with them?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I know it’s unreasonable when I say it out loud. Reopening the same spreadsheets, rereading emails line by line, checking the locks or payment confirmations over and over in a certain order until it feels “safe”... it doesn’t really make sense.\n\nBut in the moment it feels like I have to do it to calm down. And then some nights I’m still sitting at my desk hours later, and I haven’t actually finished anything.\n\nDoctor: How distressed do you feel when these unwanted thoughts occur?\n\nPatient: Very distressed. When the thought hits, it feels awful—like I might have missed one critical thing, a wrong number or an unlocked door or a bill from the wrong account, and somehow that means I’ve caused a disaster. Even if I know I already checked, my mind just won’t let go of it. It can feel overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these thoughts are something you could control if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I want to control them, but they just come in anyway. I’ll know I checked the number, or locked the back door, or paid the bill from the right account, and still the thought keeps pushing back in like, what if you missed something and caused a disaster? I can sometimes try to talk myself down, but it doesn’t feel fully under my control.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these unwanted thoughts are a part of you, or do they feel alien to your own identity?\n\nPatient: They feel pretty alien to me. I don’t see myself as someone who wants to think this way. I came in because these awful thoughts keep pushing in—like I missed a wrong number in a data release, or left the back door unlocked, or paid a bill from the wrong account—and even after I’ve checked, my mind won’t let go of it.\n\nIt feels like it’s not really “me,” but it’s happening in my head anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social activities or relationships because of these unwanted thoughts or checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yes. I’ve stopped going to things I used to enjoy, like salsa nights or kayaking, because I can’t get out the door in time. I get stuck checking and reviewing, and then it’s too late or I’m too drained to go.\n\nIt’s also hurt my work. I stay late trying to review things over and over, but I still don’t finish, so I’ve fallen behind on major deadlines.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

When the thought hits, it feels awful—like I might have missed one critical thing, a wrong number or an unlocked door or a bill from the wrong account, and somehow that means I’ve caused a disaster. Even if I know I already checked, my mind just won’t let go of it. I came in because these awful thoughts keep pushing in—like I missed a wrong number in a data release, or left the back door unlocked, or paid a bill from the wrong account—and even after I’ve checked, my mind won’t let go of it.\n\nIt feels like it’s not really “me,” but it’s happening in my head anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social activities or relationships because of these unwanted thoughts or checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yes. Even when I know I checked, my mind just won’t let go of the idea that I might have caused some kind of disaster.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel the need to do anything to make these unwanted thoughts go away?\n\nPatient: Yes. I try to make the thought quiet by checking. I’ll reopen spreadsheets, reread emails line by line, check the door locks, look at payment confirmations again and again. I do it in a certain order until it feels safe enough. Sometimes I’m still sitting at my desk hours later, and I haven’t actually finished anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do these checks in a very specific way?\n\nPatient: Yes. It has to be in a set order, or it doesn’t feel like it counts. I’ll reopen the spreadsheet, go through the emails line by line, then check the locks or the payment confirmation again. If I get interrupted or I’m not sure I did it “right,” I start over. It’s like I’m trying to get to this feeling that it’s safe, but some nights I’m still at my desk hours later and nothing is actually finished.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're unable to complete your checks in the specific way you described?\n\nPatient: Yes. If I can’t do it in the right order, I get really anxious. I’ll end up reopening the spreadsheets, rereading emails line by line, checking the locks or payment confirmations again and again until it feels safe enough. If it doesn’t feel right, I can’t settle down, and some nights I’m still at my desk hours later with nothing actually finished.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to perform these checks or rituals when you're not feeling anxious?\n\nPatient: Yes, sometimes I do. It’s not always like I’m already panicking first. Sometimes I just feel this pull to check anyway, like I need to make sure it’s safe before I can move on. So I’ll reopen the spreadsheet, reread the emails, check the locks or payment confirmations in the same order. If I don’t do it, or if it doesn’t feel right, then I start getting worked up.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these unwanted thoughts when you're feeling calm and not worried about anything in particular?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s part of what bothers me. I can be sitting there feeling pretty calm, and then the thought just pops in—like, what if I missed a wrong number in a data release, or the back door isn’t really locked, or I paid a bill from the wrong account. Even if I know I already checked, my mind won’t let go of the idea that I might have caused something serious.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and checking behaviors affecting your ability to work or take care of daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I’ve fallen behind on some major work deadlines because I’ll stay late reviewing things over and over, but somehow still not actually finish them. And outside of work, I’ve stopped making it to things like salsa nights or kayaking because I can’t get away from the checking and reviewing in time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checks are necessary to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes. In the moment it really feels like I have to do them to stop something bad from happening. I’ll reopen spreadsheets, reread emails line by line, check locks or payment confirmations over and over, usually in the same order, until it feels safe. If I don’t get that feeling, I keep going, and some nights I’m still at my desk hours later with nothing actually finished.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these unwanted thoughts are part of your own thoughts and beliefs, or do they feel like they're coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: They feel like they’re coming from me—like they’re my own thoughts—but I don’t want them. It’s not like I hear someone else saying it or anything. It’s more that my mind grabs onto this possibility, like maybe I missed a wrong number or left the back door unlocked, and even though part of me knows I checked, I can’t get it to let go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these unwanted thoughts align with your own values and beliefs?\n\nPatient: No, they don’t really line up with what I believe. I don’t want to think I’ve caused some disaster because I missed a number, or left the back door unlocked, or paid a bill from the wrong account. That’s not how I see myself. They’re just awful thoughts that get stuck, even when I know I already checked.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these thoughts are realistic or exaggerated?\n\nPatient: They’re exaggerated, I think. In the moment they feel realistic enough that I can’t ignore them, but when I step back, I know it’s not likely that one missed number or a door lock or a bill mistake means I’ve caused some huge disaster. It’s just that my mind won’t let go of that possibility.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything unreasonable about the thoughts you're having and the behaviors you're using to cope with them?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I know it’s unreasonable when I say it out loud. Reopening the same spreadsheets, rereading emails line by line, checking the locks or payment confirmations over and over in a certain order until it feels “safe”... it doesn’t really make sense.\n\nBut in the moment it feels like I have to do it to calm down. And then some nights I’m still sitting at my desk hours later, and I haven’t actually finished anything.\n\nDoctor: How distressed do you feel when these unwanted thoughts occur?\n\nPatient: Very distressed. When the thought hits, it feels awful—like I might have missed one critical thing, a wrong number or an unlocked door or a bill from the wrong account, and somehow that means I’ve caused a disaster. Even if I know I already checked, my mind just won’t let go of it. It can feel overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these thoughts are something you could control if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I want to control them, but they just come in anyway. I’ll know I checked the number, or locked the back door, or paid the bill from the right account, and still the thought keeps pushing back in like, what if you missed something and caused a disaster? I can sometimes try to talk myself down, but it doesn’t feel fully under my control.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these unwanted thoughts are a part of you, or do they feel alien to your own identity?\n\nPatient: They feel pretty alien to me. I don’t see myself as someone who wants to think this way. I came in because these awful thoughts keep pushing in—like I missed a wrong number in a data release, or left the back door unlocked, or paid a bill from the wrong account—and even after I’ve checked, my mind won’t let go of it.\n\nIt feels like it’s not really “me,” but it’s happening in my head anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social activities or relationships because of these unwanted thoughts or checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yes. I’ve stopped going to things I used to enjoy, like salsa nights or kayaking, because I can’t get out the door in time. I get stuck checking and reviewing, and then it’s too late or I’m too drained to go.\n\nIt’s also hurt my work. I stay late trying to review things over and over, but I still don’t finish, so I’ve fallen behind on major deadlines.\n\nDoctor: Have these unwanted thoughts and checking behaviors affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I mean, I’ve missed things with friends because I can’t get away from the checking and reviewing in time. I used to go to salsa nights and kayaking, and now I just don’t make it out the door.\n\nAnd with work, I stay late going over things again and again, but I still don’t finish, so I’ve fallen behind on big deadlines. It’s embarrassing, and it makes me pull back from people.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any situations or activities because of the fear of triggering these unwanted thoughts or the need to perform your checking rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes. I avoid going out sometimes because I know I’ll get caught in the checking before I leave. Like salsa nights or kayaking—I used to do those, but now I can’t get away from the reviewing and checking in time.\n\nAnd at work, I stay late going over things again and again, but I still don’t finish. I’ve fallen behind on major deadlines because of it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
